- **Scarpa F1 LT** -- 100; 100 mm; tech only; 1104 g (28). Still a reference ~1000 g boot; adequate for 200 lb skier on 120 mm skis in big terrain in soft/consistent snow; harder to ski fast on firm, learning curve from alpine boots; downhill as good as notably heavier boots; nearly effortless ROM. Top recommendation for efficiency.
- **Head Crux Pro** (Head's first efficiency touring boot) -- flex not listed; 99 mm; tech only; 1273 g. Open-throat lower + more substantial upper cuff -> above-average lateral support, slightly heavier and less ROM than rivals but better uphill than most >1300 g boots; needs neutral stance in inconsistent snow, handles speed in smooth/soft snow; lower-volume fit for class. MTN Summit / Zero G Peak Carbon for max downhill per weight.
- **Dalbello Quantum Evo** -- flex not listed; 101 mm; tech only; 1126 g (27.5). Lightest Dalbello; extremely low instep, moderately wide heel/toe; good ROM, quick-release power strap/upper buckle; among least powerful -- almost too soft fore/aft to drive 100+ mm skis in soft snow at moderate speed; most should consider other light options (fit aside).

### Touring Bindings & Climbing Skins (p.224)
Section overview: backcountry gear is confusing; beyond skis, boots, decision-making, risk assessment and trusted partners, bindings and skins matter a lot since most BC time is spent going uphill. Bindings tested back-to-back on identical skis. First four binding options match alpine bindings downhill; others far lighter for long days; others in between. "A very good time to be a backcountry skier" -- decide priorities.

**Touring bindings** (MSRP and measured weight per binding given)
- **CAST Freetour 2.0 Pivot 15** -- $779; total system 1432 g, on ski while skinning 1019 g (w/ Look Pivot 15, 115 mm brakes). CAST pioneered "hybrid" (pin toe uphill, alpine binding downhill); v2.0 based on Look Pivot 15/18 on CAST hardware (brake lock, two heel risers, swap-on touring toe); shaved weight, refined toe interface (less icing, easier swaps). Not light or fastest to transition, but descend in one of the most trusted alpine bindings; as rugged as 1.0.
- **Marker Duke PT 16** -- $699; total 1383 g, skinning 1074 g (125 mm brakes). Pin toe uphill, alpine toe flipped forward and locked or removed; transition more involved than Shift2 and slightly more finicky than Freetour 2.0 when fully removing toe; good uphill/downhill experiences, no notable issues. Duke PT 11 much lighter (~850 g uphill) if you don't need DIN 16; PT 16 burlier for frequent resort use.
- **Salomon / Atomic / Armada Shift2 13 MN** (first update to Shift) -- $649; 926 g (120 mm brakes). Addresses first-gen quirks: brakes stay up while skinning, no pre-releases, adjustable AFD stays put, toe lever works, single taller riser more functional; ~20 days across pairs; smoothed rough edges. The pick for a 10/13-DIN hybrid without swapping toe pieces.
- **Daymaker Classic Adapters & Tekdapters** -- $279 each; Classic 738 g, Tekdapters 540 g per adapter. Adapters (not bindings) let you skin in any alpine setup; no drilling; cheaper than touring bindings; heavy (plus alpine binding), worse on firm sidehill skin tracks. Classic works with nearly any boot/binding; Tekdapters need tech-toe boots but lighter, near-frictionless ROM, more compact and torsionally rigid.
- **Marker Kingpin 13** -- $699; 775 g (75-100 mm brakes). Downhill-oriented touring binding, not wildly heavy, simple, impressive power transfer; alpine-style heel more solid than any pin heel; not lightest or most elastic; prefer DIN-certified hybrids for resort laps; nicer on skin track than hybrids. Kingpin M-Werks saves ~100 g/foot.
- **Fritschi Tecton 13** -- $680; stated 630 g (100 mm brakes). Bridges hybrids and full-pin; less jarring, more powerful feel thanks to toe-pin elasticity/lateral release and alpine-style heel vertical elasticity; adjustable toe release could add safety; not Shift2/Duke PT/CAST level but step up from full-pin. Vs Kingpin: similar power, lighter, adjustable toe release. Latest has reinforced toe/heel levers and heel plate.
- **G3 ION 12** -- $664; 639 g (105 mm brakes). Straightforward functionality; better power transfer than old tech bindings like Dynafit Radical 1.0; heavier bindings here more elastic/powerful/smoother. Intuitive, reliable; simple risers/touring mode; Pozidriv-serviceable; easy to clear ice. G3 ZED lighter.
- **Dynafit ST Rotation 10** -- $650; 626 g (105 mm brakes). Rotating toe, adjustable vertical/lateral heel release, some elastic forward pressure; like Radical 2.0 it replaced but easier step-in, a touch lighter; simple transitions, great risers, fairly good downhill. Middle of bell curve; pivoting toe may offer safety benefits.
- **PLUM Karibou 12 Stopper** -- $709; 595 g (85-100 mm brakes). Rugged "freetouring" tech binding; almost all metal; excellent risers; reliable optional brake; very respectable power for full-pin; "elastic response system" under toe (8 mm spring); reliable retention/release. Brakeless Karibou 12 (stated 455 g) compelling. PLUM Pika lighter.
- **Fritschi Vipec Evo 12** -- $630; stated 580 g (100 mm brakes). Tecton toe + pin heel; lighter/simpler; less power/heel elasticity than Tecton but skis very well for pin; very intuitive; easy risers; ski/walk transition without stepping out of toe; updates: metal-reinforced toe lever, easier ski/walk lever. Midweight with adjustable lateral toe release and smoother ride.
- **Marker Cruise 12** -- $649; 482 g (105 mm brakes). Fully featured but fairly light: simple brake, forward pressure compensation, multiple risers; very comfortable/intuitive skinning; risers stay put; heel rotation easier than most (except Dynafit Ridge) without removing ski. Good suspension for weight; +10 mm heel/toe delta a bit "tip-toey." Durability TBD.
- **Marker Alpinist Free 13** (new) -- $630; 463 g (105 mm brakes). Previous Alpinist 12 with slightly wider composite around toe/heel and higher max release; same mount pattern; A/B test (12 on one ski, Free 13 on other) found no difference. Good suspension, low delta, respectable power; single riser fine on moderate tracks, can flip inadvertently. ~350 g/foot without brakes.
- **Dynafit Ridge** -- $599; 439 g. Fully featured ~400 g binding: forward pressure compensation, integrated brakes, easy step-in, "Easy-Turn-Tech" heel that reduces wrestling when rotating; no icing/durability issues; one of easiest riser configs; feels very good downhill for weight, +4 mm delta.
- **G3 ZED 12** -- $572; 358 g (no brakes/leashes). ION features in lighter package; same excellent risers; harsher and less power than heavy bindings like Tecton; comparable features to ATK Raider 12 (Raider more solid downhill, ZED cheaper without brakes). Elastic heel forward pressure, generous BSL range, multiple risers, adjustable release. Ridge / Cruise for easier transitions.
- **ATK Raider 12** (variants sold by Hagan, Majesty, DPS, others) -- $650; 352 g (97 mm brakes). Long-time reference in <400 g class; not as smooth/powerful as Tecton but no lighter binding skis notably better; fantastic risers, excellent build. Go-to for uphill efficiency + hard skiing; add ATK Freeride Spacer for power. GEAR:30 Ep.199 & 200 with ATK CEO Davide Indulti.
- **Tyrolia Almonte 12 PT** (new; Tyrolia's first tech binding) -- $439; 327 g (105 mm brakes). ~330 g with brakes; adjustable lateral release and fixed forward release (U-spring); gapless fit + optional spacer = slightly above-average power for class; mostly internal light brake functional; ~65 g savings without brakes; two easy risers. Raider/Ridge smoother; very competitive at reasonable price.
- **PLUM Pika** -- EUR469 (~$525); 305 g (no brakes/leashes). Blurs PLUM ultralight/freetouring; ~300 g, nearly full metal, adjustable vertical/lateral heel release (not U-spring), heel block reduces boot-ski gap (swappable for light brake); easy step-in; single riser higher than Alpinist's. Downhill like ZED / Almonte; below Raider / Karibou. Karibou for higher speeds.
- **Salomon MTN Pure & MTN Summit 12 BR** -- $599-649; MTN Pure 298 g (no brakes/leashes), MTN Summit 410 g (110 mm brakes). Pure for low weight, simplicity, ease; Summit more downhill-oriented/fully featured with elastic forward pressure and adjustable lateral release (less vague power, fewer vibrations; not quite Raider level). Both excellent risers, easy step-in.
- **Dynafit Superlite 150** -- $649; 176 g (no brakes/leashes). Very light yet capable of fairly fast big turns in forgiving snow; half the weight of most 5-13 release bindings; 200 lb reviewer in stiff boots drove big skis in most BC conditions; better toe retention than previous Superlite 175 Z12; low ~6 mm delta. Compelling without brakes/BSL adjustment; with those added, weight approaches fuller bindings.
- **Grizzly GR98** -- $649; 98 g. For racers/ski mountaineers, but one reviewer enjoys it more broadly; unique toe takes practice; remarkably solid; no pre-release in two seasons; 1/3 to 1/4 weight of 300-400 g bindings; release "8" or "10" versions; lateral release in crash but race-style heel with single low riser. Not for pushing limits downhill.

**Climbing skins -- Mohair plush**
- **Contour Hybrid Pure** -- two-layer Hybrid adhesive very easy to rip with skis on, holds well, tackiness revived by washing; mohair offers glide/grip blend (less efficient but grippier than next two); not as thin/packable as Pomoca Free Pro 2.0 / Race Pro 2.0; tail clips less secure than G3 (esp. twin tails). Hybrid Mix for grip/durability; Hybrid Speed for efficiency.
- **Pomoca Climb Pro Mohair** -- versatile all-rounder cold to warm; water-resistant coating/membrane -> less water absorption/glopping; good glide/grip mix (less uphill efficiency than Race Pro 2.0); excellent Pomoca adhesive (tackier than Contour Hybrid, still easy to rip), effective hardware. More grip/durability than Free Pro 2.0 / Race Pro 2.0; Pomoca Climb 2.0 more compact, affordable, grippier.
- **Pomoca Race Pro 2.0** -- Paul Forward's all-time favorite skin; most efficient glide here, excellent packability, adhesive holds yet peels easily; not durable, struggles on steepest/iciest tracks; only limited narrow widths and skimo attachments (unless uncut roll) -- used happily on wider skis anyway. Pomoca Free Pro 2.0 for wider skis.

**Climbing skins -- Mix plush**
- **Black Diamond GlideLite Mix STS** -- reliable; better glide than nylon, more rugged than mohair; similar to Pomoca Climb 2.0 (BD glue stickier; Climb 2.0 packs smaller, lighter); latest STS hardware lower profile, less snow creep, fits many shapes. Free Pro 2.0 / Minimist Glide more packable.
- **Contour Hybrid Mix** -- same easy-rip Hybrid adhesive on grippier, more durable plush; much easier to rip than G3/BD glue (no cheat sheets needed); revive glue at home; hardware less secure than G3 but packs much smaller than G3 Alpinist; still performing after many seasons.
- **G3 Alpinist+ Glide** -- most rugged G3 (large plastic end inserts); same plush as Minimist Glide: good grip, notably better glide than nylon; TPU tip doesn't pack small; great hardware for wide variety of ski shapes; tacky glue. Minimist for minimal hardware; G3 "Universal" plush for icy tracks.
- **G3 Minimist Glide** -- sleek low-profile hardware that stays secure (good for odd/wide tips); middle-of-road mix grip/glide; very tacky glue (Blister prefers Pomoca/Contour Hybrid); not for the hardest-on-gear users; carbon end inserts prevent snow creep better than nearly all similarly thin skins.
- **Kohla Vertical MixMohair** (used for many brands' pre-cut skins) -- fairly thin/light, between Pomoca Climb 2.0 and Free Pro 2.0, but stiffer -> less packable, durable, less snow creep; mid grip/glide; above-average water resistance; hardware like Pomoca; adhesive less sticky than G3, (continues).
  - (Kohla cont.) ...not as easy to rip as Contour Hybrid -- functional middle ground; good alternative to BD GlideLite Mix if you want less tacky glue.
- **Montana Adrenaline** (Montana also makes tuning machines) -- glueless "FusionTec" adhesion, stickiness between Contour Hybrid and Pomoca -- very easy to rip yet stays on; mohair/nylon mix, thickness/weight like Pomoca Climb 2.0 (maybe lighter); typical mix grip/glide. Montana MontaNyl for slick slopes.
- **Pomoca Climb 2.0** -- "beginner-friendly" but does a lot very well; better glide and packability than nylon without big price or grip penalty; easier to rip without detaching. One of most versatile, easy to recommend. BSMP Rover for icy tracks/budget; Free Pro 2.0 more efficient/packable.
- **Pomoca Free Pro 2.0** -- thin efficient race-like plush with attachments/sizes for big freetouring skis; packs smaller than most, glides really well, wide widths; respectable grip esp. on big skis; hardware works on nearly every ski; adhesive easier to rip than BD/G3, stickier in tricky conditions than Contour Hybrid. Pricey; thin build doesn't love rocks/downed trees.

**Climbing skins -- Nylon plush**
- **Big Sky Mountain Products Rover** (BSMP also re-glue service) -- durable and affordable; heavier/bulkier but glides as well as any nylon skin, plenty of grip; still performing after hard multi-season abuse; Pomoca-like hardware. BSMP Endurance mix skin competitive so far.
- **Black Diamond Ascension Nylon STS** -- at every trailhead; solid all-round; glides less than mohair/mix (slightly better than G3 Alpinist+ Universal); lighter/smaller than G3 nylon Alpinist due to minimal updated hardware (sleeker, better snow-creep resistance). GlideLite Mix for better glide.
- **G3 Alpinist+ Universal** -- grippy plush, rugged snow-resistant hardware; "fishscale" plastic tip piece didn't noticeably reduce deep-snow trail-breaking effort, but TPU tips reduce snow creep; moderate weight, Rover more packable. For pack-stowers wanting firm-snow grip over glide; good for skis whose tips/tails don't hold hardware well. Minimist series more packable.
- **Montana MontaNyl** -- traditional glue (BD-like stickiness); solid all-round nylon: grippy, adequate glide, repels moisture, durable; packs smaller than G3 Alpinist Universal; hardware fine on most skis (bar-style for pointy/asymmetric tips). Montana MontaMix more efficient; Adrenaline easier ripping.

**Blister Spectrums -- Climbing Skins**
- Best Glide -> Worst: Pomoca Race Pro 2.0, Pomoca Free Pro 2.0, Contour Hybrid Pure, Pomoca Climb Pro Mohair (these 4 similar); Montana Adrenaline, Pomoca Climb 2.0, BD GlideLite Mix, G3 Alpinist+ Glide, G3 Minimist Glide, Contour Hybrid Mix, Kohla Vertical MixMohair (these ~8 similar); BSMP Rover, Montana MontaNyl, BD Ascension Nylon, G3 Alpinist+ Universal (these 4 similar, worst glide).
- Most Grip -> Least: BD Ascension Nylon, Montana MontaNyl, BSMP Rover, G3 Alpinist+ Universal (similar); G3 Minimist Glide, G3 Alpinist+ Glide, Pomoca Climb 2.0, BD GlideLite Mix STS, Montana Adrenaline, Kohla Vertical MixMohair, Contour Hybrid Mix (7 similar); Pomoca Free Pro 2.0, Pomoca Climb Pro Mohair, Contour Hybrid Pure, Pomoca Race Pro 2.0 (4 similar, least grip).
(Photo: Justin Bobb, Taos.)

### Snowboards & Splitboards (p.236)
Section overview: collection of reviewers' favorite snowboards, splitboards, bindings, and splitboard skins, with lots of time on most; explains what sets each apart. Specs given: stated dimensions (tip-waist-tail width), sidecut radius, profile, lengths, base, core. Boards appear arranged roughly by waist width.
(Photo: Andrew Forward, Chugach AK.)

**Snowboards**
- **Weston Riva** (women's) -- 288-246-283 mm (149); 8.05 m avg; rocker/camber/rocker; 145-157; sintered 4000; paulownia/poplar + carbon stringers + fiberglass. Easy to recommend from progressing riders to playful veterans; directional shape, setback, wide nose = surfy/playful in pow; moderately stiff for lighter riders, women-specific flex easy inputs, quick response; more versatile/stable at speed than softer pow surfers; quick corrections and smooth arcs on hardpack. Accessible, adaptable all-rounder.
- **Deviation Works Outlier v4.0** -- 295-251-289 (158); 8.3 m; rocker/camber/rocker; 140-164; sintered; ash/poplar/purple heart + fiberglass. Different take on do-everything: excellent groomer carver, more on-piste excitement than most jack-of-all-trades; extremely impressive edge hold and high-angle stability; fast base; needs attention and dynamic riding to pivot off piste/big moguls (Juggernaut easier). One of most impressive carving boards recently tested; viable all-mountain for torsionally rigid precision lovers.
- **Rossignol Juggernaut** -- 297-253-297 (158); 6.7-7.3 m avg; 5% tip rocker / 90% camber / 5% tail rocker; 149-162 (incl. wides); sintered 7500; paulownia/poplar + carbon, kevlar, fiberglass. High-end park board traits in hard-charging all-mountain chassis: high-speed stability, edge hold, exceptional pop; centered stance = stiff precise freestyle tool for firm/big features; bindings back = aggressive all-mountain. Like Amplid UNW8 but true twin and mellower camber -> more freestyle-friendly, easier slow.
- **Arbor Bryan Iguchi Pro Camber** -- 296-254-295 (162); 9-8.1-9 m; parabolic camber; 153-167w; sintered plus; poplar/paulownia/bamboo + fiberglass. Versatile freestyle-inspired board for intermediate to expert riders hucking all terrain; traditional camber + "Grip Tech" contact points = great edge hold on firm; directional-looking tip/tail keep nose up in shallow pow but twin feel; 3-degree lead-edge bevel at tip/tail = catch-free butters/spins/slashes. Fairly stiff for class (softer than previous Jones Ultra Mountain Twin, stiffer than Weston Range / Season Aero); snappy at moderate pace, stable fast.
- **Nitro Squash** -- 301-255-295 (159); 8.4/7.6 m; traditional camber; 156,159,163; formula HD base; poplar/beech + fiberglass. Blends surf-inspired and directional freeride: lively, versatile on firm or pow; rails powerful turns with impressive edge hold (full camber, progressive sidecut, moderate swallowtail) like Jones Hovercraft on piste; exceptionally poppy; stubby swallowtail playful yet supportive on jumps (more than Weston Japow / Season Forma); nose floats; not chattery when firm. Great first swallowtail for versatility seekers.
- **Gnu Hyper** -- 300-256-295 (160); 8.3 m; Mervin C2x (camber/rocker/camber); 151-160; sintered; aspen/paulownia + carbon stringers + fiberglass. Soft, snappy, very playful; light-footed nimble riding; C2x (rocker underfoot, camber outside feet) surfy, maneuverable; 5 mm taper barely noticeable switch; approachable flex; not for big turns in variable or high-angle firm carving. For progressing intermediates, park rats going all-mountain, advanced riders wanting playful freestyle without speed. Iguchi Pro for composure.
- **Amplid UNW8** -- 302-258-300 (159); 8.0 m; traditional camber; 156,159,163; sintered 7 HD; poplar/paulownia + paper honeycomb + antiphase pad + carbon & fiberglass. Demanding reviewer favorite: solid, poppy, lightweight for advanced full-camber riders who go fast; no rocker + stiff flex demand precise input in rough/grabby snow; less playful than softer boards. Particularly precise, excellent stability for low weight, incredible pop; excels in open terrain; uniquely powerful yet agile for aggressive riders.
- **Season Aero** (board) -- measured 304-258-300 (160); 7.7 m; rocker/camber/rocker; 145-163w; sintered 4000; poplar/paulownia + fiberglass. Extremely well-rounded freeride board that's playful everywhere; directional shape, snappy flex, pointed nose surf pow; camber underfoot and long effective edge responsive on firm; lively/jibby like Weston Range, not best through chop; floats better than Range. Top do-everything contender. Season Nexus softer, surfier.
- **Weston Range** -- 306-258-306 (161); 7.85/8/7.85 m; rocker/camber/rocker; 152-164w; sintered 4000; bamboo/poplar + carbon stringers + fiberglass. Many riders quickly enjoy it; directional twin, camber underfoot, moderately stiff = super playful with enough spine for soft tracks/variable; doesn't blast crud like Juggernaut / UNW8 but snappier, less demanding; intuitive edge hold (vs serrated sidecuts) encourages speed, big hits, playful riding. Aero floats better; Range capable on firmer/shallower days; safe daily driver.
- **Amplid Pentaquark** -- 306-260-303 (158); 8.6 m; traditional camber; 158 only; sintered 7; poplar/paulownia + paper honeycomb tip/tail + antiphase pad + carbon & fiberglass. Still one of best carving boards tested; race board + all-mountain hints; light yet damp (Antiphase); long effective edge (short tip/tail) = excellent firm edge hold; smoother, more predictable at high edge angles than serrated-sidecut boards; makes bland snow fun without perfect technique. Others more versatile.
- **Season Nexus** (board) -- 302-260-296 (158); 7.8 m; rocker/camber/rocker; 143-164; sintered 4001; poplar/paulownia + fiberglass. Season's widest-range model; not most stable/snappy/floaty but makes "blah" resort days fun; rider dictates turn shape and style; easy for newer riders (mellow torsional flex); "medium" predictable flex ideal for side hits, transitions, jibbing; refreshingly coherent design vs niche boards; pairs well with Forma or Weston Japow.
- **WNDR Alpine Belle Aire** -- 304-260-294 (159); 7.8 m (all lengths); tip rocker / 5 mm directional camber; 148-164; sintered; aspen + algal PU foam stringers + recycled inserts + fiberglass. Distinct: exceptional edge hold, respectable stability; excels in pow, handles chop/variable well thanks to damp non-chattery build (algae-derived PU; inserts from repurposed production waste). On firm prefers dynamic unweighting/hopping to whip around -- not easiest. Pow/carving-oriented; split version Belle Tour.
(Photo: Jed Doane, Furano Japan.)
- **Ride Commissioner** -- 307-261-297 (162); 11 m; tip rocker / camber; 153-165; sintered 4000; paulownia + carbon stringers + titanal/carbon topsheet + carbon & fiberglass. Maximum high-speed composure; blasts tracked pow and set-up snow with near-unrivaled calm; best for dense heavy snow to fresh corduroy (not blower or refrozen crud); enough float for moderate pow; predictable edge hold for on/off piste. One of most demanding here -- needs input and strength.
- **TahoeLab Directional** (handmade in Lake Tahoe) -- 303-261-296 (158); 7.8 m; rocker/camber/rocker; 143-180 (incl. wides); sintered durasurf; bamboo + carbon & fiberglass. From Freeride World Tour to fast resort laps: very stiff, very stable; one of best for edge grip (like Pentaquark) but tip/tail rocker make it more versatile in big mountains than Pentaquark / Commissioner; still snappy and playful; minimal chatter; very durable after many rock strikes. For experts; Season Nexus for less aggressive.
- **Amplid Snommelier** -- 323-262-292 (166); 9.25 m; rocker/camber/flat; 166 only; sintered 7; poplar/paulownia + carbon stringers + paper honeycomb tip/tail + fiberglass. Narrow niche; favorite for arcing huge turns in soft snow; wide blunt nose, 166 length, vintage swallowtail keep nose afloat and ease back leg; surprisingly light. Less maneuverable in tight terrain than Japow, Forma, or stubby surf boards (Jones Storm Chaser); exceptionally fast in blower or wet spring pow. For big open terrain. Japow for tight terrain.
- **Weston Japow** -- 317-262-292 (159); 9-6.9 m; rocker/camber/flat; 154-169; sintered 4000 electra; bamboo/poplar + carbon tail stringers + fiberglass. Still a favorite surf-inspired board for fresh pow or corduroy; traditional swallowtail encourages aggressive wide turns yet nimble in tight terrain; vs Snommelier slower in open but much more maneuverable in trees; vs shorter Rossignol XV Sushi faster/more stable, less niche; playful, pops off natural hits. Top pick for fluid surf ride + adaptability.
- **Jones Hovercraft 2.0** -- 311-263-294 (156); 9 m; rocker/camber/rocker; 144-164; sintered 8000 UHMWPE; poplar/paulownia + "Re-Up" stringers + flax & fiberglass. "Re-Up Tech" presses used snowboards into stringers reinforcing nose (GEAR:30 Ep.270). Familiar shape: large rockered nose for float and crud-busting, tapered subtly split tail for maneuverability; mid stiffness, progressively stiffer toward back (short powerful tail). Aggressive riders may want more at speed in variable/airs. Excels surfing pow and carving groomers.
- **K2 Excavator** -- 318-264-298 (154); 6.1-7.1 m; camber; 142-162; sintered carbon-infused 5500; bamboo/aspen/paulownia + carbon stringers + fiberglass. Different take on volume-shifted: snappy precision plus high-speed stability; 20 mm taper, setback stance, stout carbon-reinforced tail = plows chop, carves hardpack, bounces in soft snow; short but stiff dependable tail; ride shorter (reviewer sized down 7-8 cm) yet stable at speed; back-foot style in chop; long low-angle and tight trench carves.
- **Jones Storm Wolf** -- 303-265-295 (162); 8.8 m; rocker/camber/rocker; 150-162; sintered 8000; paulownia/bamboo + flax & basalt stringers + carbon, innegra, fiberglass. Directional freeride surf blend; one of stiffer swallowtails tried -> cuts heavy wet pow, still snappy; designed for speed, very stable, super-G carves; shape between long pow guns (Snommelier) and stubby volume-shifted (XV Sushi) -> float + agility. For advanced/expert prioritizing big-turn stability.
- **Ride Warpig** -- 316-265-306 (151); 5.4-6.5 m; rocker/flat/rocker; 142-158; sintered 4000; aspen/bamboo/paulownia + impact binding plates + carbon stringers + fiberglass. Volume-shifted freestyle board that's versatile; stubby mid-wide, stable at speed, easy to drive through crud; skate-style feel for ollies/spins; substantial low-rise tail more stable than pin-tailed rivals; mid-wide suits bigger feet, stability at high edge angles; not quickest edge-to-edge; stiffer end as park board for big jumps.
- **Arbor Carter Camber** (Mark Carter signature) -- 309-266-301 (158); 8.0-8.9 m; camber w/ offset tip & tail "fenders"; 149-161; sintered; poplar/paulownia/bamboo + carbon stringers + fiberglass. Volume-shifted all-round freestyle platform; wider silhouette adds float and reduces toe/heel drag; moderate flex lively and nimble; camber + Grip Tech = pop and decent grip but less smooth/secure carving than traditional sidecuts; fenders prevent catching; "medium to medium-soft" flex -- accessible, limited at speed.
- **Rossignol XV Sashimi** (Xavier De Le Rue signature; middle of collection) -- 331-266-279 (160); 7.2-8.6 m; 30% rocker / 60% camber / 10% rocker; 152-160; sintered 7500; poplar + perimeter urethane strip + fiberglass. Tapered, maneuverable freeride board keeping much of XV Sushi's float/playfulness with much more high-speed dependability; nose blasts chop; front-foot on hardpack or back-foot steering in trees; substantial tail for snap/landings (not a freestyle board). Playful side of Sushi-to-XV spectrum; Hovercraft 2.0 for more stability.
- **Soul Stick Shift Elite** -- 325-267-290 (159); 9-6.9 m; camber w/ 3D contouring; 159 only; sintered 7700; poplar/paulownia + carbon stringers + fiberglass. Excellent volume-shifted swallowtail for aggressive riders used to big freeride boards; length helps at speed and eases learning curve; one of most damp boards tested last season -- nearly impossible to unsettle; setback stance for back-foot pivots; big 3D nose floats and blows through chop. Not a daily driver (not great in air or tight low-angle terrain) but a blast with room to run.
- **Season Forma** -- measured 319-267-308 (153); 7 m; rocker/camber/rocker; 144,153,159; sintered 4000; poplar/paulownia + fiberglass. Between short stubby slashers and long guns -- one of safer bets in surf-inspired class; relatively fast in deep snow, ample float, exceptionally playful slashing/railing/sending; like Weston Japow but slightly surfier, easier to throw, a bit slower in bottomless; decent groomer edge hold. Good entry to swallowtails.
- **Ride Superpig** -- 301-270-291 (154); 5.6-6.9 m; tip rocker / camber; 142-158; sintered 4000; aspen/bamboo/paulownia + carbon stringers + carbon-infused glass. Tuned-up Warpig: adds camber, stiffer -> less playful, more stable, better edge hold; excellent float but less maneuverable in deep snow than Nexus/Aero or more tapered stubby boards; very sharp snappy groomer carves; comfier at speed than Warpig. For stubby-shape fans wanting stiffer, more precise, more stable than XV Sushi.
- **Jones Storm Chaser** (updated) -- 324-275-304 (152); 6.9 m; tip rocker / camber; 142-157; sintered 8000; paulownia/bamboo + flax & basalt stringers + fiberglass. Favorite "pow-groomer" board; size down several cm; lots of volume in short nimble swallowtail; surprisingly fast, easy to plane, more maneuverable on low-angle slopes than Forma / Sushi; not as fast in bottomless or stable in chop as longer swallowtails (Storm Wolf, Snommelier, Japow); remarkable on-piste carver (added camber between bindings), like Nitro Squash but easier to slash.
- **Rossignol XV Sushi** -- 340-276-282 (145); 8-9.5 m; 30% rocker / 60% camber / 10% rocker; 145 only; sintered 4400; poplar + urethane strips + fiberglass. Short fat agile pow surfer: 340 mm nose, ~6 cm taper, 88 mm tail; front-foot-heavy in pow, calm in soft chop, loses composure in firm crud at speed (vs Japow/Forma). Blast on groomers -- extreme width makes carving effortless without toe/heel drag. Use a stiff binding. Sashimi / K2 Excavator more versatile.
- **K2 Special Effects** -- 333-284-313 (stated); 6.8 m (152); tip rocker / flat; 140-152; sintered 4000; bamboo/aspen/paulownia + carbon stringers + fiberglass. One of widest boards ever ridden; stiff flex, huge surface area, 8 mm nose spooning, flat-to-rocker = very easy initiation, surfy; nimble and buoyant in deep snow -- perfect for Hokkaido trees; less tapered/more symmetric than XV Sushi (more intuitive for less directional riders); not damp in crud; fun to carve, passable in packed pow / dust-on-crust.
(Photo: Justin Bobb, Taos.)

**Splitboards**
- **Jones Solution Split** -- 311-254-298 (164); 9.3 m; rocker/camber/rocker; 154-169w; sintered 9900; paulownia/poplar + flax & basalt stringers + fiberglass. Adaptable for soft BC snow in varied terrain; floats very well, big turns, accessible in tight trees; advanced riders find speed limit in firm/variable (edge hold, chatter) -- better for less experienced; easy to kick-turn; dual-serrated edges grip well split and solid. Jones Stratos more playful/better on firm.
- **Prior Khyber Split** (lightweight XTC construction tested; heavier fiberglass version; custom) -- 314-256-287 (160); 8.0 m; rocker/camber/rocker; 150-170; sintered 4001 durasurf; aspen/poplar + carbon. Highly maneuverable, floaty, versatile; wide nose + narrower tail = float, less leg fatigue; especially poppy in XTC -- pillows, features, tree lines. Not as fast as directional swallowtails (Never Summer Swift, Chimera Unicorn Chaser) but more playful/balanced; lighter than Venture Paragon, similar performance.
- **Jones Ultralight Stratos** -- 305-256-295 (159); 7.5 m; rocker/camber/rocker; 156,159,161w; sintered 9900; paulownia/poplar + koroyd + carbon & fiberglass. 3D-spooned tip/tail minimize catchiness of light splits while keeping hard-snow response; middle-of-road vs stable Voile Skyline and surfy NS Swift; slight deep-snow bias, a bit of BC freestyle; livelier than Solution / Hovercraft 2.0; not a charger (softer than most carbon splits); standard Stratos Split cheaper but much heavier.
- **Chimera Unicorn Chaser** -- 312-257-284 (159); 8.9 m; rocker/flat/camber; 159 only; sintered 4001 durasurf; aspen/poplar + carbon stringers + fiberglass. Surfier approach without being limited in variable; surfs/pivots under front foot, camber under back foot bites on hardpack; moderate tail cutout = fast in pow, nimble; less surfy than stubby Sushi but more stable/versatile; uphill slightly better than Swift (lighter, narrower nose, camber).
- **Weston Backwoods Carbon Split** -- 312-259-294 (160); 7.7/6.9/8 m; rocker/camber/rocker; 152-167w; sintered 4000 electra; paulownia/bamboo + vertical aluminum stringers + carbon. Do-everything biased to bigger lines and higher speeds; directional, floats; stout flex edges well in variable; 1-split-quiver contender vs Venture Paragon / Prior Khyber -- stiffest of three, still light. Jones Ultralight Hovercraft surfier. Reliable uphill (grip, easy kick turns); one of most dependable when opening up turns.
- **Stranda Shorty Backcountry** -- 313-260-299 (164); 8.6 m; rocker/camber/rocker; 153-169; sintered durasurf 4001; ash/poplar + paulownia tip inserts + carbon stringers + fiberglass. Versatile pow tool despite name; long wide nose floats, rises after big drops (better landings than short tails); softer nose than Unicorn Chaser / Swift -> less for charging tracked/firm; camber good for carving and slashing pow/slush/corn. This season lighter paulownia nose inserts -> lighter, more balanced on skintrack (previous gripe). Accessible soft-snow surfer.
(Photo: Justin Bobb, Santa Fe backcountry.)
- **WNDR Alpine Shepherd Tour** -- 314-260-294 (162); 7 m (all lengths); subtle tip rocker / camber; 150-166; sintered; aspen + algal PU foam stringers + recycled inserts + fiberglass + algal sidewalls. Unique shape; broad nose and long effective edge = excellent steep-terrain edge hold, easy pivoting; "medium" flex, strong short tail for pow lift; damper/more composed than Jones Solution with volume-shift swallowtail playfulness; tight radius for snapping in narrow spots; setback tapered shape adds stability. Not for blasting crud or many features. Tighter-turning than Belle Tour.
- **Never Summer Swift Split** -- 319-263-299 (162); 8.32 m; camber/rocker/camber; 157,162,167; sintered durasurf XT 5501; NS superlite wood + carbon & fiberglass. Freeride traits in surf-inspired silhouette; like Hovercraft 2.0 / Unicorn Chaser: surfy, floats; longer tail and weight = more stability at speed/variable; handles airs/landings well for class. Less nice on skintrack -- "Original Rocker Camber" rocker underfoot problematic on icy trails; Ultralight Hovercraft lighter and better split.
- **Venture Storm Split** (named for Storm Peak, San Juans; solid version too) -- 314-265-305 (162); 7.92 m; rocker/flat/rocker; 152-167; sintered p-tex; poplar/ash + fiberglass. Venture known for burly durable builds (lighter over years); rocker/flat/rocker + 8 mm taper = freeride shape that floats, turns easily in trees, reliable skinning without slippage of other rockered splits. Paragon more freestyle; Euphoria surfier. Very versatile.
- **Cardiff Goat Pro Carbon Split** (cheaper heavier "Enduro" build too) -- 301-265-290 (162); 11 m avg; 40% rocker / 60% camber; 150-166; sintered graphite; poplar/paulownia + titanal stringers + carbon. High-end freeride, playful, poppy; ~3085 g @162; durable and predictable in rough snow; slightly tapered/setback for float and stability yet balanced in air, great edge hold; one of poppier splits despite stiffness. Particularly versatile for big days.
- **Venture Paragon Split** -- 315-265-307 (159); 7.61 m; rocker/flat/rocker; 149-168; sintered p-tex; poplar/ash + fiberglass. Great 1-split quiver; new version more blunted tips/tails, modestly directional; strong in chop/crud, maneuverable in trees, switch/spins, dependable grip on traverses and icy couloirs; not light but among most durable; damp for moderate flex; more stable than Stratos/Solution; more freestyle than Backwoods/Powgoda. Multi-season investment.
- **Jones Ultralight Hovercraft Splitboard 2.0** -- 316-267-299 (160); 9.3 m; rocker/camber/rocker; 152,156,160; sintered 9900; paulownia/poplar + koroyd + carbon & fiberglass. Directional, surf-inspired, for carving and deep pow; large spooned nose glides, catch-free; Ultralight build ~2.7 kg @160 with no apparent durability/performance sacrifice (pricey); flex matched to shape -- stable fast yet flickable; "Traction Tech" serrations on both ski edges for firm skin tracks. Similar to discontinued Amplid Millisurf (longtime favorite).
- **Lib Tech Orca Split** -- 311-267-302 (159); 8 m; camber/rocker/camber; 150-159; sintered; paulownia/aspen + fiberglass. Excels in pow, surprisingly capable in variable; volume-shifted, floats better than generalists like Paragon; long supportive tail = great landing gear (cliffs, sketchy tree exits). Sashimi / Venture Storm softer/surfier; Orca more stable at speed. Quiver killer. Previous board for lower weight.
- **Cardiff Powgoda Pro Carbon Split** (Enduro build cheaper) -- 314-272-294 (158); 8.4 m; 50% rocker / 50% camber; 148-162; sintered graphite; poplar/paulownia + titanal stringers + carbon. Light (2984 g @158), soft, surfy daily driver for fresh snow with solid all-mountain feel; nose rocker + moderately tapered tail = very good float; not as agile as stubby surfers; progressive sidecut: back-foot for tight trees, front-foot for big turns. Goat less directional, more stable.
- **Voile Skyline** -- 310-272-299 (162); 9.5 m; rockered tip / camber; 154-172; sintered; paulownia + carbon & fiberglass. Top pick for stability and precision in consequential terrain; versatile for advanced/expert (pivots, floats in soft snow); most impressive in poor conditions -- very damp (burly TPU sidewalls), minimal chatter, secure on firm; not easy to pivot/skid on firm; long radius favors long fast turns. Like a much damper, more stable, wider Jones Solution.

**Snowboard bindings**
- **Nidecker Supermatic** -- flex 8/10; nylon, fiberglass; M (5-8), L (8.5-10.5), XL (11-13). Standout for easy entry: hinged design -- step down on highback to lock into ride mode and tighten straps; flip rear lever to release. Very efficient (preference-dependent); precision riders still tighten buckles; softer than 8/10; want burlier buckles; new stiffer Supermatic Carbon to test. No binding-specific boots needed.
- **Ride A-8** -- 8/10; aluminum, nylon, TPU; M (6-10), L (10.5+). Reliable stiffer all-mountain without ultra-rigid carbon; nylon highback powerful for big freeride boards yet torsional flex for surfy feel; reversible ankle strap adjusts ankle flex (stiffer option comfortable/responsive); simple durable straps; cushy 2.5-deg canted footbed comfortable yet supportive.
- **Arbor Cypress** -- medium-stiff; aluminum, fiberglass, plant-based strap plastic, EVA; S/M (7-9), M/L (9-11), L/XL (11-13+). Highly adjustable mid-stiff freeride binding for intermediates up; plant-based plastic straps wrap foot but allow jibby give; responsive lean, comparable chassis to A-8 / Supermatic; X-shaped baseplate and foam inserts preserve board flex (feel like Now SkateTech); adjustable strap lengths and heel cup; durable for price.
- **Jones Apollo Pro** -- 10/10; nylon, fiberglass, carbon, flax; M (7.5-10), L (10+). Stiffest Jones; precise edge control and power; Now Bindings' SkateTech chassis (center pivot) reduces fatigue, increases edge response; stiff asymmetric carbon/flax highback yet smooth transitions; for stiff/wide boards; ankle straps flip "freeride mode" to "surf mode" and softer bushings for forgiveness; versatile enough as daily setup.

**Splitboard bindings**
- **Spark R&D Arc ST Pro** -- medium; aluminum, carbon-reinforced nylon, pebax; XS-L. Reviewer favorite; ~560 g per binding (M) with few compromises; performs like Arc ST, no durability flags after multiple seasons; carbon-infused asymmetric highback marginally stiffer, surfy do-everything feel. Paired with Spark Pucks (more stance adaptability than Voile Pucks); Ibex ST Pro crampon ~120 g each. Top pick for lighter, surfier medium flex.
- **Voile Light Speed** -- medium; aluminum, steel, nylon; S (4-7.5), M (8-10.5), L (11+). Fairly stiff, rugged; "Quick Lock" touring bracket like Spark's Tesla T1; pins are a solid metal piece mounted to board (less chance of falling out vs Spark's pins in binding); many screws/bolts -- repairable but more to monitor; more built-in adjustability; slightly more prone to icing; some prefer its risers and straps; ~750 g/binding, comparable to Spark Surge ST.
- **Spark R&D Surge ST** -- stiff; aluminum, fiber-reinforced nylon, plastic; XS-L. Stiffer Arc ST for higher speeds, bigger boards/riders; "Pillow Line" semi-elastic plastic straps conform, shed snow, more durable than stitched straps; Tesla T1 system easy and durable, improved with "ST" hardware. Not as stiff as Karakoram Straightline / X Carbon but more versatile and better value.
- **Spark R&D Surge ST Pro** -- stiff; aluminum, carbon-reinforced nylon, pebax; XS-L. Spark's highest-end; ~70 g/foot lighter than Surge ST without durability/performance loss; ST touring brackets, Whammy Bars, Ibex Crampons in overmolded thermoplastic for smoother, longer-lasting rotation; asymmetric "Rip 'N' Flip" highbacks for lateral movement/natural stance; drives big boards without harshness; binding of choice for many reviewers.

**Splitboard climbing skins**
- **Kohla Peak Mixmohair Splitboard Universal** -- updated with waterproof "Fiber Seal" backing and less tacky "Smart Glue"; packable, glide, moisture management; adapts to nearly any split; hardware more versatile than BD standard; 65% mohair / 35% nylon -- great grip-to-glide, surprisingly grippier than G3 nylon Universal on ice and pow; secure clips, but some tail clip durability issues. One of favorite all-round skins.
- **G3 Splitboard+ Universal** (replaces Alpinist) -- 100% nylon; revised tail clips fit more tail shapes; tip/tail connections more reliable than Kohla Peak but poorer glide; "Glide" and "Speed" variants via G3 "Craft" discount program; improved glue consistency/longevity. G3 Splitboard LT lighter.
- **Black Diamond Ascension Splitboard STS** -- safe choice for grip/durability; streamlined hardware; nylon plush unchanged for years, one of most versatile, slightly better glide than G3 Splitboard+ Universal; multidirectional tail clip adjustment eases cutting; secure (maybe less versatile for odd shapes than G3). Kohla Peak for packability/glide.
- **Jones Nomad Splitboard Skins** (made by Pomoca; universal trim-to-fit or precut for Jones splits 2017+) -- 70% mohair / 30% nylon, versatile like Kohla Peak; less efficient than G3 "Glide" on low angles but grips better steep/icy; Nomad Pro adds Pomoca Glide treatment; precut tail clips need tail notch -- works extremely well on Jones splits like Ultralight Hovercraft 2.0; no glue issues with care; thin, light, packable (less bulky than G3/BD).

### Helmets & Goggles (p.259/265)
Section overview -- Helmets: no one should ski/ride without a properly fitting helmet; personal story: Jonathan Ellsworth lost footing bootpacking on a summer tour, went over a cliff, tomahawked through a boulder field, broke his neck; wearing a helmet, survived (he and witnesses believe he wouldn't have otherwise), surgery, skiing six months later (Blister Podcast #47 and #48). Not all helmets protect equally (GEAR:30 "Current State of Helmet Tech" series), but any is far better than none. Goggles: better still is not crashing; seeing well in bright, cloudy, storm conditions makes skiing safer/more fun; lens and lens-swap improvements trickling down to cheaper models; alternatives also discussed.
(Photo: skier Cody Townsend, Blister Summit 2024.)

**Helmets** (MSRP / measured weight)
- **Pret Vision X (women's) / Fury X (men's)** -- $220; 473 g (M). Packed with features: ample ventilation, multi-shell with Mips, antimicrobial foam. Also tested Pret Cirque X, Lyric X2, Cynic X2 -- all pair with wide range of goggles; Vision/Fury lighter and more vented than Lyric/Cynic (good values). Fans of Pret fit, comfort, durability; Vision/Fury for low weight + ventilation.
- **Sweet Protection Igniter 2Vi Mips** -- $250; 721 g (M/L). Mid-lineup with high-end safety: "2Vi" = hard outer shell + shock-absorbing foam + 2-piece Mips liner, integrated to reduce weight/increase comfort; dial fit adjuster, Fidlock magnetic buckle, multiple vents. Trooper 2Vi more rugged/less vented; Looper Mips cheaper, lower profile.
- **Glade Boundary** -- $199; 421 g (M). Lightweight, many vents with independently opening front/rear; good for human-powered skiing; dial works with bare hands and mittens; Fidlock buckle; Mips. Flagship features at lower price.
- **Giro Owen Spherical** -- $240; 521 g (L). Premium, sleek minimalist; two reviewers with very different heads got secure fits; few vents but effective airflow when moving; pretty light; Mips Spherical (two-piece foam shell, quieter, more comfortable than plastic liner); Fidlock; quick-dry liner; skate-style look.
- **Salomon MTN Lab** -- $249; 379 g (M). Long-standing BC favorite; helped launch multi-norm certified helmets (ski, mountaineering, bike); very light, works with most goggles, looks like normal ski helmet; more coverage than climbing helmet for rock/ice-fall approaches; vents well; thinner "summer" liner. Others better for daily lift laps.
- **Atomic Four Amid Pro** -- $220; 487 g (M). Atomic "Amid" system: multiple dual-density pads deform to reduce multi-directional (incl. rotational) impacts; Atomic claims up to 40% more protection than industry standards; Fidlock, soft antimicrobial liner, dial fit; few vents but good airflow moving; light -- compelling 50/50 helmet, esp. if stashed on pack for ascent.
- **Smith Summit Mips** -- Smith's most touring-oriented; certified to ski and mountaineering standards; headlamp strap routing, bungee cables for pack lashing, compact BOA fit, many vents; thin earpad-less knit liner, (continues).
  - (Smith Summit Mips cont.) $235; 404 g (M). Liner designed for bare head, beanie, or cap; low-profile coverage, light materials less ideal for resort; doesn't pair well with some goggles (curved upper frames). Purpose-built BC helmet; Salomon MTN Lab if fit doesn't work.
- **POC Fornix Mips** -- $200; 490 g (XL/XXL). Go-to for years; most valued features without complexity or high price; fairly light, large adjustable vents -> lift and human-powered; functional dial; traditional buckle easy with gloves. Similar category to Giro Owen / Atomic Four Amid Pro. POC Obex Mips for less skate-style look.

**Goggles** (MSRP)
- **Anon M5 / M5.S** -- $300 incl. 2 Perceive lenses + MFI face mask. Anon pioneered magnetic lens swaps; M series spans face sizes; M5 mid-size with "Flat Toric Lenses" (toric distortion/field-of-view benefits with flatter cylindrical look); ridiculously easy yet secure swaps; MFI mask with magnetic nose piece. Anon Sync cheaper.
- **SHRED Gratify** -- $220 incl. 1 CBL 2.0 lens. Fairly large cylindrical goggle with excellent optics; "Contrast Boosting Lens 2.0" (developed with MIT; GEAR:30 Ep.272 with founder Ted Ligety); no quick-swap or photochromic but single "CBL 2.0 Ice" lens excellent sunny to graybird; works with Medium helmets; no old-cylindrical distortion.
- **Julbo Launcher** -- $200-320 incl. 1 lens. First cylindrical Julbo with "SuperFlow Pro" (pop lens out slightly to vent); REACTIV photochromic (tested REACTIV 0-4 High Contrast) adapts to huge range incl. wet low-light storms; best with REACTIV since no easy swap; "medium" fit. For fogging issues / one-lens setups.
- **Smith Squad XL Imprint 3D** -- $250 incl. 2 ChromaPop lenses + custom 3D-printed frame (from at-home phone face scan). Managing editor's best-fitting goggle ever; hook-and-slot lens change somewhat tedious; broad coverage for medium-large faces. Standard Squad XL ($155) a top value pick.
- **Glade Fathom+** -- $199 incl. Reveal Photochromic + low-light lens. Glade known for versatile affordable photochromics; flagship toric design with quick magnetic swap; mid fit. Glade Adapt 2 (cheaper, broader cylindrical, no magnets) for taller/longer faces.
- **Pit Viper Proform** -- $170 incl. 2 lenses. New to goggles; fully magnetic yank-and-slap swaps; slightly less contrast than pricier goggles (subtle); big frame esp. height (like Anon M4) but fits M and L helmets. French Fry cheaper without magnets.
- **Dragon DX3 Plus OTG** (new) -- $160 incl. 2 Lumalenses. Pared down but impressive optics; traditional swap; Lumalens contrast better than most low-end goggles; "medium" fit; "armored" venting resists snow clogging; choose version with bright + low-light lens. Swiftlock series for easier swaps.
- **SPY Marauder Elite x Happy Boost** -- $310 incl. 2 Happy Boost lenses. SPY's top model; toric lens; Happy Boost contrast/saturation better than most high-end goggles; six-magnet quick swap; medium-large frame, thick frame/foam may protrude from thin-brim helmets. Standard Marauder with Happy Boost $250.

### Apparel (p.265)
Section overview: apparel matters (soaked shell on pow day, steam-room pants on skin track); a small subset of apparel tested (more on site); from breathable touring pieces to burly resort garments; organized by male reviewers (first two pages) vs female reviewers (following pages); most available in both or unisex.
(Note: TOC lists Apparel p.259 and Helmets & Goggles p.265, but in the body Helmets & Goggles precede Apparel.)

**Male-reviewer-tested kits** (MSRP; measured weight; fabric)
- **Flylow Dante Jacket & Magnum Pant** -- $340 / $375; 888 g (L) / 659 g (S); jacket 2L Surface (155D), pant 3L Tactic & Surface (150D). Dante has zonal insulation for mid-winter resort laps, simplifies layering, reliably waterproof; Magnum uninsulated burly shell with nearly full-length side zips (huge vents, on/off with boots). Flylow often roomy but Magnum average/slightly slim at hips. Great values; durable over multiple seasons.
- **Norrona Tamok Gore-Tex Jacket & Pant** -- $579 / $479; 797 g / 842 g (L); 2L PFC-free Gore-Tex (ePE) with 70D & 150D recycled face fabrics. "Backcountry freeride" line but great for resort; burly, fully featured; roomier than most Norrona, extra jacket length; not light/very breathable but ~50 days with zero issues; new PFC-free ePE membrane on par with old ePTFE so far (GEAR:30 Ep.264 on PFCs).
- **Stio Figment Jacket & Bib** -- $499 / $479; 858 g / 897 g (M); 3L PeakProof (150D recycled). "Substantial": rugged stout face fabric, heavy, slightly below-average breathability -> not for lots of touring; excellent everyday resort kit; many pockets; notably long, slightly roomy; protective in wet spring storms. Next kit softer.
- **Trew Cosmic Jacket PRIMO & Eagle Pant PRIMO** -- $499 / $459; 906 g / 920 g (M); 3L PNW laminate (recycled). Cosmic PRIMO an all-time favorite; burly yet supple, less crinkly; 100+ days without a tear, still dry; Eagle Pant notably durable -- reviewer who slices cuffs of every pant in 25-50 days has no brake-snag holes; many pockets, loose relaxed fit; Eagle = lower-cut TREWth Bib PRIMO.
- **Beringia St. Elias Jacket & Bib 2.0** -- $675 / $675; 603 g / 650 g (M); 3L Täsmä (nylon face + knit polyester backer), air-permeable. One of most breathable, comfortable waterproof laminates; keeps out moisture/wind; supple; fairly roomy fit; favorite for touring but works in resort; short cuff reinforcements. Tsurugi anorak lighter with same fabric.
- **Patagonia Storm Shift Jacket & Pant** -- $499 / $399; 802 g / 869 g (M); 2L PFC-free Gore-Tex (150D) with fleece lining (all recycled). Early adopter of PFC-free Gore-Tex; like Tamok on paper but slimmer/shorter and warmer via thin R1 Air-like fleece; new insulated Storm Shift exists; Patagonia SnowDrifter / Stormstride / Upstride better for BC; solid resort kit simplifying layering.

**Female-reviewer-tested kits**
- (Page header repeats Trew Cosmic PRIMO; women's Stella PRIMO is the counterpart per Best Of.)
- **Flylow Lucy Anorak & Foxy Insulated Bib** -- $380 / $430; 694 g / 717 g (L); 3L Tactic (recycled); 40 g Greenloft recycled insulation in bib. Insulated Foxy keeps fit/features, water-resistant, warmer but versatile with big vents; new Lucy Anorak: generous pockets, roomy hood, supple burly fabric. Flylow shifted all pieces to PFC-free for 24/25.
- **Armada 3L Pavara Jacket & Rayleigh Bib** -- $479 / $379; 719 g / 709 g (L); 3L 20K/20K laminate (76% recycled face). Flagship women's 3L kit; lighter and more supple than many 3L kits; less breathable than air-permeable Strafe but well vented; great 50/50 kit; true to size, relaxed/baggy; lots of pockets.
- **Jones MTN Surf Jacket & Bibs** -- $449 / $399; 867 g / 787 g (L); 2L laminate (75D recycled). Jacket subtly insulated, breathable, soft stretchy lining -- resort-oriented (insulation, weight); uninsulated bibs impressively breathable, stretch panels at hips/thighs, rugged ripstop at cuffs/knees, 20K rating seems accurate -- great 50/50. Less warm than Flylow Foxy Insulated/Lucy combo; good near freezing.
- **Strafe 3L Meadow Jacket & Willow Half Bib** -- $609 / $449; 520 g / 545 g (S); jacket 3L eVent DValpine (40D), bib 3L Recon ELITE (63D). For mostly-lift riders who also tour or want lighter, more breathable kit; plenty of venting, above-average breathability; flattering, feature-rich, sleek; Willow suppler, stretchier, slimmer than Strafe Scarlett Bib (more rugged alternative); half-bib boosts breathability.
- **Outdoor Research Hemispheres II Jacket & Bib** -- $649 / $649; 493 g / 571 g (M); 3L Gore-Tex (70D + C-Knit backer) with 2L Gore-Tex Stretch panels. OR's high-end stretchy, light, protective shells; 50/50 but backcountry-leaning; warm resort days or layered for cold. Armada Pavara/Rayleigh more rugged.
- **Rab Khroma Kinetic Jacket & Bib** -- $400 / $380; 560 g / 579 g (size 10); 3L Proflex (stretch face + knit backer), claimed 20K. Touring-oriented: mobility, breathability, comfort; feels like a softshell, breathes better than most 3L shells; fine in resort unless hard on gear; spring go-to; slimmer fit, fewer pockets.

## 4. Blister 'Best Of' Awards (p.271)
Framing: single out exceptional products (other gear may suit you better). Rules: (1) if two products perform similarly, pick the one with more logged time -- not about new, about good; (2) only gear they can vouch for (criticizes publications selling awards via ad deals or awarding barely tested gear); (3) no company paid for inclusion or awards.
- **All-Mountain Skis**: Folsom Spar 88 (sub-90 whole-mountain); K2 Mindbender 89Ti (precise on piste, great off piste); Salomon QST 92 (rare maneuverability/playfulness at ~90); Line Blade (just plain fun, tight carves, even park); Line Optic 88, 96, 104 & 114 (fusion of playfulness, suspension, versatility; new 88 adds firm-snow precision); K2 Mindbender 99Ti (one of most well-rounded on market); J Skis Masterblaster (smooth suspension without burly/demanding ride); Salomon Stance 102 (supportive, encourages aggression yet agile); Volkl Mantra 102 (suspension/stability plus tight technical terrain); Icelantic Nomad 100, 106 & 112 (new versions livelier, more agile without losing composure); Moment Countach 110 (worked for more reviewers than almost any ski).
- **Freestyle Skis**: Season Kin (jibby park ski + tight carver); Armada Edollo (another year; stable on big jumps and fun on small features); Faction Studio 2 (formerly Mana 2; "aggressive-but-dynamic"); K2 Reckoner 102 & Reckoner KF (most playful yet cohesive/versatile; KF -- described here as "new 114mm-wide" -- ludicrously poppy with high ceiling); RMU Apostle 106 & Apostle 114; Rossignol Sender Free 110 (burly, damp, precise on playful platform).
- **Powder Skis**: Prior CBC (agile/surfy freestyle pow + burly damp build for big technical terrain); Moment Wildcat (OG "playful charger"); Rossignol Sender Free 118 (Blackops 118 replacement; managing editor: charging chop on it is "the closest thing I've felt to transcendence"); Folsom Rapture (Paul Forward's current guiding favorite); Heritage Lab HB122 (one of most fun all-round soft-snow skis; accessible flex, playful, stable).
- **Women-Specific Skis**: Blizzard Black Pearl 94 (BP97 suspension, more accessible/lively); Volkl Secret 96 & Secret 102; K2 Mindbender 99Ti W; Icelantic Maiden 102 & 108; Wagner Summit 106 (instantly intuitive for female and male testers; class-leading 50/50 versatility); Coalition Snow Rafiki (one of best women's resort pow skis).
- **50/50 Skis**: Atomic Bent Chetler 120 & Bent 110; 4FRNT Hoji (split personality: tight trees and big fast arcs).
- **Touring Skis**: Majesty Superwolf (spring go-to; downhill-to-weight ratio, predictable); 4FRNT Raven (calm, composed in tricky BC snow).
- **Touring Bindings**: ATK Raider 12.
- **Ski Boots**: Tecnica Zero G Tour Pro & Tour Pro W (new even better); Atomic Hawx Ultra XTD 130 BOA (eerily close to fixed-cuff downhill; 50/50); Tecnica Cochise 130 & Cochise 115 W; Scarpa F1 LT (~1 kg class); Tecnica Zero G Peak Carbon; Salomon S/Lab MTN Summit (stiffest/most progressive sub-1200 g boot tested); Atomic Hawx Ultra 130; Lange Shadow 130 LV & Shadow 115 LV W (distinct excellent flex, power transfer); Atomic Redster CS 130 (founder's go-to resort boot; "thoughtfully" low-volume); ZipFit Gara Liners (nearly indestructible, outlast shells, re-customizable cork for years).
- **Snowboarding**: Weston Japow; Voile Skyline Splitboard; Venture Paragon Splitboard; Cardiff Powgoda Pro Carbon Splitboard; WNDR Alpine Belle Aire; Spark Surge ST Pro & Arc ST Pro splitboard bindings.
- **Apparel -- Outerwear**: Trew Stella (women's) & Cosmic PRIMO jackets; ORTOVOX 3L Deep Jacket & Pants (not reviewed in apparel pages; favorite for features, looks, versatility); Flylow Lucy Jacket & Lucy Anorak; Norrona Tamok Gore-Tex Pants (founder's go-to; great waist adjustment, durability); Flylow Baker Bib (incredibly durable resort bib).
- **Apparel -- Midlayers**: Patagonia Micro Puff & DAS Light Hoodies (PlumaFill warmth-to-weight, water-resistant); Patagonia R1 TechFace Hoody (breathability + weather resistance; put on and keep on); Patagonia Nano-Air series (Nano-Air, Nano-Air Light Hybrid, revived Nano-Air Light Bottoms).
- **Apparel -- Baselayers**: CW-X Stabilyx Joint Support 3/4 Compression Tight (worn nearly every ski day by a reviewer; knee support); Nuyarn wool baselayers (merino benefits plus durability, thermal regulation, stretch).
- **Goggles**: Anon WM1, M2, M3, M4, M5 & Sync; SPY Marauder Elite x Happy Boost; Glade Adapt 2; Julbo Launcher.
- **Accessories**: Scott Team Issue Pole (none broken in a decade); GoggleSocs; DB Ski Bag (all-time favorite; rolls up, hands-free wheeling strap); Skida Brim Hat; Wells Lamont HydraHyde Mittens (value; all-leather, durable as mitts costing 2x+); Hestra Leather Fall Line Gloves (durable, supple, drier climates); BCA Float E2-35 (best airbag pack reviewed; supercapacitor Alpride E2 as good as E1; Side Stash pockets, radio spot); Mammut Barryvox S beacon (Paul Forward's all-time favorite; current even better).

## 5. Index of Skis, back matter
- Index of Skis (p.279-281) lists every ski by brand with page numbers (repeats across sections; Best Of page refs 272-274). Brands covered: 4FRNT, Armada, Asnes, Atomic, Black Crows, Black Diamond, Blizzard, Cardiff, Coalition Snow, DPS, Dynafit, Dynastar, Elan, Faction, Fat-ypus, Fauna, Ferreol, Fischer, Folsom, FullSend, G3, Head, Heritage Lab, Icelantic, J Skis, K2, Kastle, La Sportiva, Liberty, Line, Majesty, Meier, Moment, Moonlight, Movement, Nordica, Norse, Northland, Parlor, Peak, Prior, Renoun, RMU, Romp, Ronin, Rossignol, Salomon, Scott, Season, Shaggy's, Stockli, Volkl, Wagner, Weston, Whitedot, WNDR Alpine, ZAG. (Index lists "Deathwish 112: 163" for the Moment Deathwish; Mantra 102 entry has no page number printed.)
- Thank-you page: to Blister+ members (support enables rejecting revenue that would undermine honesty); reviewers (Kara Williard, Kristin Sinnott, Dylan Wood, Paul Forward, Justin Bobb, Eric Freson, Mark Danielson, Noah Eckhouse, Andrew Forward, Jed Doane, Sascha Anastas, Luke Koppa, Jonathan Ellsworth); photographers (Taylor Ahearn, Charlie Renfro, Rob Suminsby, Zach Husted, Justin Bobb, Dylan Wood, Kristin Sinnott, Kara Williard, Jonathan Ellsworth, Luke Koppa). Staff: Luke Koppa (Managing Editor & Graphic Designer), Kara Williard (Producer & Senior Editor), Dylan Wood (Senior Editor), Mark Danielson (Copy Editor), Kristin Sinnott (Senior Editor), Jonathan Ellsworth (Editor-in-Chief).
- Final page: "in loving memory of Eric Freson, #torksmash" (photo of Dylan Wood, Crested Butte).

## 6. Notable themes / what's new this year
- **Memorial and safety framing**: the 10th edition is dedicated to reviewer Eric Freson (killed in a spring 2024 backcountry accident); editor's letter urges annual written safety commitments (Avy courses, WFR) while still "picking spots to push." The helmet section repeats Ellsworth's broken-neck story.
- **Massive line overhauls**: Blizzard replaced Bonafide/Brahma/Cochise with the new Anomaly 88/94/102 and refreshed Black Pearl (84/88/94; BP94 replaces BP97); Nordica updated Enforcer (94/99/104 replacing 100 and 104 Free) and Santa Ana (92/102); Volkl launched M7 Mantra, renamed Katana 108 as Mantra 108, redesigned Blaze (94/104/114), Peregrine replaces Deacon; Dynastar overhauled M-Pro (92 W/98 W/94 Ti/100 Ti) and tweaked M-Free 108 lengths; Line dropped "Blade" from Optic, added Optic 88, overhauled Vision (96/104/114) and made Pandora fully unisex (99/106); Icelantic redesigned Nomad (100/106/112) and Maiden (102/108); K2 revamped Reckoner (102 tweaked, new 110, 124, KF) and Omen (90, Team replacing Poacher); Faction renamed Mana as Studio; Atomic added Maverick 105/115 CTI, Maven 103 CTI, Backland 108 W/109; DPS revived Wailer (90/100/107, cheaper Utah-made construction); Rossignol revived "Soul" (Sender Soul 102) and replaced Blackops 118 with near-identical Sender Free 118; Armada replaced ARV 116 JJ with ARV 112, launched ARW 112 (successor to JJ) and Declivity X 102/108; Head launched Crux touring line; Kastle launched Paragon freeride line; Liberty Radian replaces Evolv; Fischer launched Nightstick freestyle and The Curv GT series.
- **Lighter-but-strong vs. weight**: Blister notes mass still correlates with stability ("not exactly thrilled" that More Stable skis are no longer heavier), yet several brands now make strong lighter directional skis (Head Kore, Majesty, Stockli Stormrider); Chargers section celebrates brands acknowledging weight is valuable.
- **Sustainability**: WNDR Alpine algae-based PU; G3 R3 100% recyclable skis; Fischer Curv GT 85 Redefine (~36% CO2e reduction); Volkl recycled PET stringers; Ferreol flax instead of fiberglass; Jones Re-Up stringers from used boards; Atomic Bent footprint tweaks; Dynastar M-Free construction; PFC-free Gore-Tex ePE and Flylow going fully PFC-free.
- **Hybrid/tech trends**: Shift2 fixes first-gen quirks; CAST Freetour 2.0; tech-toe-only fixed-cuff boots (Fischer RC4 DYN, K2 Recon 140); BOA closures proliferating (Fischer, K2, Salomon Shift Alpha, Atomic Hawx Prime); new boot brands (Phaenom; Kastle since 22/23); Rossignol Vizion step-in boots; Tyrolia's first tech binding; Head's first efficiency touring boot; Tecnica Zero G Tour Pro gen 2 and new women's Pro.
- **Blurred categories**: repeated message that playful vs directional pow, forgiving vs stable, frontside vs all-mountain, and touring vs AT boots are increasingly blurred; many skis appear in multiple sections; Spectrums intended to locate skis relative to peers.
- **Standing favorites**: Mindbender 99Ti, Wildcat, Bent Chetler 120, V-Werks Katana (unchanged for years), Raven, Superwolf, Scarpa F1 LT, ATK Raider 12 remain references; Blister reiterates "think before you buy" -- older gear still performs.
